text: default page 8
| Methods and Hydrogeologic Data from Test Drilling\x20
| and Geophysical Logging Surveys in the\x20
| Lawrenceville, Georgia, Area
| By Lester J. Williams, Phillip N. Albertson, Donna D. Tucker, and Jaime A. PainterAbstract
| Thirty-two wells, ranging in depth from 180 to 630 feet,\x20
| were used to study the bedrock lithology, fracture, and water-
| yielding characteristics in the Lawrenceville, Georgia, area.\x20
| These data were compiled to determine what geologic struc-
| tures, if any, contribute to the development of increased perme-
| ability and high ground-water yield in the area. Methods used in\x20
| this study include test-well drilling, geophysical logging and\x20
| borehole-camera surveys, flowmeter surveys, aquifer testing,\x20
| packer testing, and water-level monitoring.
| Water-bearing fractures identified in open boreholes of\x20
| wells include: joints, open joints, and zones of joint concentra-
| tion; foliation partings and major foliation openings along folia-
| tion and layering of the rock; dissolution openings along mineral\x20
| infillings; and irregular voids and fractures. Most of the joints\x20
| observed in the boreholes appeared as tight hairline fractures\x20
| and typically were not significant water-bearing zones. Moder-
| ate to small amounts of water — from 1 to 5 gallons per minute\x20
| (gal/min) — are produced from open, steeply-dipping joints and\x20
| zones of joint concentration. Foliation partings and major folia-
| tion openings, which form “foliation parallel-parting systems”\x20
| in the area, yielded large quantities of water to open boreholes.\x20
| Foliation partings typically yielded from 1 to 15 gal/min, with a\x20
| maximum value of about 63 gal/min. In some boreholes, groups\x20
| of foliation partings form significant water-bearing zones yield-
| ing as much as 50 gal/min. Major foliation openings yield sub-
| stantially more water than the smaller foliation partings, with a\x20
| typical range from 50 to 100 gal/min. Major foliation openings\x20
| are the primary water-producing features responsible for high-
| yield wells in the area. In a few wells, dissolution openings\x20
| along mineral infilled joints or veins had yields as much as\x20
| 35 gal/min, indicating the potential importance of dissolution\x20
| features in the bedrock.
| Flowmeter surveys, aquifer tests, packer tests, and water-
| level monitoring provided additional hydrologic information on\x20
| water-bearing fractures in the study area. These data were used\x20
| to help confirm the depth and yield contribution from various\x20
| types of water-bearing fractures, indicate the hydraulic charac-
| teristics of these fractures, and show the hydraulic response of\x20
| the aquifer system to pumping.
| Collectively, the data from this study indicate that foliation\x20
| parallel-parting systems, consisting of discontinuous zones of\x20
| foliation partings and major foliation openings, strongly influ-
| ence the yields of wells in the Lawrenceville area. Wells tapping\x20
| these systems are capable of sustaining large ground-water\x20
| withdrawals for extended periods of time, as indicated from the\x20
| continuous operation of the Rhodes Jordan Wellfield since 1995.\x20
| Open-hole water levels, flowmeter surveys, and preferential\x20
| drawdown parallel to foliation and compositional layering all\x20
| indicate a general hydraulic confinement of foliation parallel-
| parting systems, and indicate a strong lithologic and structural\x20
| control on the development of these water-bearing fracture systems.
| Foliation parallel-parting systems are easily identified in\x20
| boreholes using geophysical methods described in this report.\x20
| The yield potential of foliation parallel-parting systems within\x20
| an individual topographic basin or several topographic basins\x20
| can be large, depending on the areal extent of the water-
| bearing zones and the interconnectivity of these zones with\x20
| sources of recharge.
| Introduction
| There is a great need to assess the ground-water resources\x20
| of the crystalline-bedrock aquifer in the Piedmont and Blue\x20
| Ridge physiographic provinces of Georgia (fig. 1). Ground\x20
| water in this region could be used to supply water to small com-
| munities or supplement the larger surface-water systems in\x20
| times of drought, for emergency supplies, or as a long-term con-
| tinuous source to increase the capacity of these systems to meet\x20
| future supply demands. In any given area, however, little infor-
| mation generally is available to assess the potential of develop-
| ing a water supply from these complex aquifer systems. Despite\x20
| this lack of information, large ground-water supplies1 have\x20
| been developed in many parts of the region (Carter and Herrick,\x20
| 1951; Cressler and others, 1983; Herrick and LeGrand, 1949;\x20
| Radtke and others, 1986; Thomson and others, 1956).1Large ground-water supplies typically have been defined in the literature as 25 gallons per minute (gal/min) or greater. \x20
| In this report, 75 gal/min or greater generally is used to indicate a large supply.

| Introduction 3The U.S. Geological Survey (USGS), in cooperation with\x20
| the City of Lawrenceville, began collecting lithology, fracture2,\x20
| yield, and water-level data from bedrock wells during Decem-
| ber 1994 to investigate the geology and ground-water resources\x20
| of the Lawrenceville area. Major objectives of the Lawrence-
| ville study were to: (1) evaluate the regional hydrogeologic set-
| ting, (2) delineate and characterize subsurface discontinuities\x20
| and fractures that control aquifer permeability, and (3) monitor\x20
| the response of the bedrock ground-water system to local\x20
| ground-water pumping. To accomplish these objectives, the\x20
| USGS completed local- and site-specific studies between 1995\x20
| and 2002. The local studies entailed compiling detailed hydro-
| geologic information on private and publicly owned wells in the\x20
| vicinity of Lawrenceville and preparing a 1:24,000 geologic\x20
| map of the study area (Chapman and others, 1999). The site-
| specific studies included a test-well drilling program and col-
| lection of geophysical logging data needed to characterize the\x20
| distribution of lithology and fractures in the subsurface. Detailed\x20
| subsurface lithology, fracture, and yield data — which are a\x20
| focus of this report — were compiled to determine what geologic\x20
| structures, if any, contribute to the development of increased\x20
| permeability and high ground-water yield3 in the area.
| Purpose and Scope
| This report describes the methods used and data resulting\x20
| from test drilling and geophysical logging surveys conducted\x20
| between December 1994 and October 2001. Included in this\x20
| report are:
| • methods of collecting and analyzing data;
| • fracture data showing the depth, nature, and yield\x20
| of different types of water-bearing fractures;
| • aquifer-test data showing the yield of wells and the\x20
| direction and magnitude of drawdown;
| • packer-test data showing the hydraulic response\x20
| among high-yield fractures; and
| • water-level data showing fluctuations in ground-water\x20
| levels in response to seasonal variations in precipitation\x20
| and from local ground-water withdrawals.
| The test-drilling program included field observations during\x20
| drilling, lithologic sampling, and descriptions of rock samples.\x20
| Geophysical logging and borehole-imaging techniques were\x20
| used extensively to characterize lithology and fractures in open-
| bedrock wells. The scope of this report includes lithologic and\x20
| borehole geophysical logs from 32 wells, flowmeter surveys\x20
| from 12 wells, aquifer-test data from 9 wells (total of 10 tests),\x20
| packer-test data from 1 well, and continuous water-level data\x20
| from 26 wells. Images of subsurface fractures and other struc-
| tures are included to document the types of bedrock fractures\x20
| and small-scale structural features common in the study area.
| Description of the Study Area
| The 44-square mile (mi2) study area includes the City of\x20
| Lawrenceville and adjacent areas in Gwinnett County (fig. 1).\x20
| The study area is in the Piedmont physiographic province—an\x20
| area underlain by igneous and metamorphic crystalline rocks.\x20
| In Georgia, the Piedmont lies between the Valley and Ridge and\x20
| Blue Ridge provinces to the north and the Coastal Plain prov-
| ince to the south (fig. 1). Topography consists of low hills and\x20
| moderately entrenched stream valleys that range in altitude\x20
| from 780 feet (ft) to 1,170 ft. Lawrenceville is on a major drain-
| age divide that separates the Yellow and Alcovy River Basins\x20
| (fig. 1). To the west, the Lawrenceville area is drained by Red-
| land Creek, Pew Creek, and tributaries of the Yellow River.\x20
| To the east, the Lawrenceville area is drained by Shoal Creek\x20
| and tributaries of the Alcovy River.
| Water Use
| In Gwinnett County, water use totaled about 90.5 million\x20
| gallons per day (Mgal/d) during 2000 (Fanning, 2003). Of\x20
| this amount, only about 0.5 Mgal/d were withdrawn from\x20
| ground-water sources. Of the 0.5 Mgal/d withdrawn from\x20
| ground-water sources, 80,000 gallons per day (gal/d) were\x20
| used for commercial purposes, 390,000 gal/d for public supply,\x20
| and 10,000 gal/d for livestock. From the surface-water sources,\x20
| only 80,000 gal/d were used for livestock and the remainder\x20
| (90 Mgal/d) was used for public supply.
| Lawrenceville currently (2003) uses about 2.5 Mgal/d for\x20
| public supply, of which about 120,000 to 140,000 gal/d is\x20
| obtained from ground-water sources (Mike Bowie, City of\x20
| Lawrenceville, oral. commun., 2002). The Rhodes Jordan\x20
| Wellfield (fig. 1) currently (2003) is the only operating active
| wellfield in the Lawrenceville area, and has two production\x20
| wells that are alternately pumped at rates of 200–250 gallons\x20
| per minute (gal/min) for 10 or more hours per day. The well-
| field was refurbished in the early 1990s and has been in contin-
| uous operation since 1995. Historically, ground water also was\x20
| withdrawn from another well site located on Maltbie Street\x20
| (fig. 1). A replacement well for the Maltbie Street well was\x20
| drilled in the late 1990s but was never put into production\x20
| because of local ground-water contamination near that site.\x20
| More recently, six of the test wells drilled for this study were\x20
| converted into production wells by the City of Lawrenceville.\x20
| These new production wells, combined with wells at the Rhodes\x20
| Jordan Wellfield, are capable of producing a combined esti-
| mated yield of approximately 2 Mgal/d (Mike Bowie, City of\x20
| Lawrenceville, oral commun., 2002).2In this report, “fractures” refer to openings along foliation planes, joints, and brittle fractures related to faulting.
| 3In this report, “high ground-water yield” refers to areas where wells have a reported yield of 70 gal/min or greater. \x20
| Cressler and others (1983) defined “high-yield” as 25 gal/min or greater.
text: default page 11
| 4 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaGeologic Setting
| The geologic setting of the Lawrenceville area varies sub-
| stantially from location to location, primarily because of the\x20
| complex structural deformation of the bedrock. The general\x20
| history of structural deformation includes thrust faulting, large-
| scale folding and faulting, partial melting of preexisting rocks\x20
| forming migmatites, and syntectonic to post-tectonic intrusion\x20
| of granitic bodies. Atkins and Higgins (1980), Higgins and oth-
| ers (1984, 1988, 1998), McConnell and Abrams (1984), and by\x20
| Chapman and others (1999) described the geology of the region.\x20
| Crawford and others (1999) described the revision of the strati-
| graphic nomenclature for the geologic units in the study area.
| Chapman and others (1999) divide the various rock types in\x20
| the Lawrenceville area into seven principal lithologic units:\x20
| amphibolite, biotite gneiss, button schist, granite gneiss, mag-
| netite quartzite, quartzite/aluminous schist (quartzite/schist),\x20
| and diabase dikes (fig. 2, table 1). The principal lithologic units\x20
| represent mappable rock groups correlated based on dominant\x20
| rock types. Differences in weathering and fracturing in the prin-
| cipal lithologic units produce a wide variation in the hydrologic\x20
| properties of these units.
| The principal lithologic units penetrated in wells drilled in\x20
| the study area include amphibolite, biotite gneiss, button schist,\x20
| granite gneiss and quartzite/schist (fig. 2). For the most part, all\x20
| of these units, excluding the granite gneiss, are compositionally\x20
| layered, consisting of several to many rock types in each unit.\x20
| The compositional layering varies from finely laminated (indi-
| vidual layers only tenths of inches thick) to thinly layered (typ-
| ically less than 6 inches) and, in fewer instances, thickly layered\x20
| (typically greater than 6 inches). All of these units also may be\x20
| massive; in particular, large bodies of massive granite gneiss\x20
| and biotite gneiss crop out throughout the area.
| The amphibolite consists of fine- to medium-grained, dark-
| green to greenish-black, massive to finely laminated, horn-
| blende-plagioclase and plagioclase-hornblende amphibolite\x20
| (Chapman and others, 1999). This unit, interlayered with biotite\x20
| gneiss, is penetrated by many high-yield wells in Lawrenceville\x20
| and forms a significant water-bearing unit.
| The biotite gneiss consists of medium- to coarse-grained,\x20
| gray to grayish-brown, to dark-gray biotite-rich gneiss (Chap-
| man and others, 1999) and forms a significant water-bearing\x20
| unit where it is interlayered with amphibolite. This unit gener-
| ally is schistose in texture and locally contains lenses and pods\x20
| of hornblende-plagioclase amphibolite.
| The button schist consists of medium- to coarse-grained,\x20
| dark-gray to brownish-gray garnet schist with interlayered\x20
| biotite gneiss and scarce amphibolite (Chapman and others,\x20
| 1999). This unit generally has a sheared texture, and there is\x20
| evidence that the button schist was derived from shearing of\x20
| biotite gneiss (Higgins and others, 1998). The button schist is\x20
| named for its weathering characteristic that yields mica concen-
| trations that resemble “buttons.” The button schist generally is\x20
| not considered a significant water-bearing unit in the\x20
| Lawrenceville area; however, several high-yield wells derive\x20
| water from this unit.
| The granite gneiss consists of a light gray to white, medium-
| grained, muscovite-biotite-feldspar-quartz gneiss. This unit\x20
| generally is a poor water-bearing unit in the area.
| The quartzite/schist consists of quartz-rich schist, musco-
| vite schist, and layers of resistant quartzite. The quartzite/schist\x20
| is penetrated by only two wells. No large water-bearing zones\x20
| were identified in the quartzite/schist at these two wells.\x20
| Diabase and magnetite quartzite are not penetrated by any\x20
| wells used in this study and are not discussed further.
| Large-scale structural features in the Lawrenceville area\x20
| include a northeast-southwest trending, doubly-plunging\x20
| synform in the central and eastern part of the study area, and an\x20
| east-west-trending synform in the western part of the study area\x20
| (fig. 2). Through the main part of the city, the lithologic units\x20
| generally strike east-west and dip gently to the south. Each\x20
| principal lithologic unit shown in figure 2 is bounded by thrust\x20
| faults; the outcrop patterns are typical of those that result from\x20
| eroded open folds.
| Hydrogeologic Setting
| Ground water fills joints, fractures, and other secondary\x20
| openings in bedrock and pore spaces in the overlying mantle of\x20
| soil, saprolite, alluvium, and weathered rock. In this report, the\x20
| soil, saprolite, alluvium, and weathered rock are collectively\x20
| referred to as regolith.
| Ground-water recharge to the regolith and underlying bed-
| rock is mainly through infiltration of precipitation at the land\x20
| surface. The infiltrating water collects in the regolith and\x20
| recharges the bedrock fracture system underlying it. Because\x20
| regolith has a much higher storage capacity than bedrock, the\x20
| regolith can be conceptualized as a ground-water reservoir or\x20
| “sponge” that feeds the underlying bedrock. Joints, weathered\x20
| zones, dissolution openings, and zones of brittle faults in bed-
| rock and combinations of these features also can store a sub-
| stantial quantity of water.
| The storage capacity and depth of weathering of the\x20
| regolith/bedrock fracture system is influenced largely by differ-
| ences in the weathering character of various rock types. These\x20
| variations in weathering are most apparent from geologic field\x20
| mapping; rocks more resistant to weathering were observed to\x20
| form pavement rock outcrops in streams and some hilltops;\x20
| whereas, rocks less resistant to weathering were observed to\x20
| form thick saprolite. The depth of casing of bedrock wells\x20
| (table 2) also is a general indication of the depth of weathering.\x20
| In the Lawrenceville area, saprolite generally is thicker on feld-
| spar-rich rocks and thinner on quartz-rich rocks. The biotite\x20
| gneiss unit (Chapman and others, 1999) is particularly suscep-
| tible to deep weathering and typically forms a thick saprolite\x20
| above the bedrock. Mafic rocks (such as amphibolite), because\x20
| of the general lack of feldspar, typically are characterized by\x20
| thin saprolite development. In layered rocks, saprolite forms\x20
| between layers of otherwise hard bedrock. In the Lawrenceville\x20
| area, this occurs where chemically less resistant rock (such as\x20
| biotite gneiss) is compositionally layered with more resistant\x20
| rock (such as amphibolite).

| 6 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaTable 1. Description of principal lithologic units in the\x20
| Lawrenceville, Georgia, area (from Chapman and others, 1999).
| Amphibolite unit (a) – Fine- to medium-grained, dark green to\x20
| greenish-black, ocher weathering, massive to finely layered, locally\x20
| laminated, locally pillowed, locally chloritic, commonly garnetifer-
| ous, locally magnetite-bearing, generally pyrite-bearing, generally\x20
| epidotic, hornblende-plagioclase and plagioclase-hornblende\x20
| amphibolites with minor amounts (generally less than a very small\x20
| fraction of 1 percent) of fine- to medium-grained, generally amphib-
| ole-bearing, granofels. The final weathering product of the amphibo-
| lite is a very characteristic dark red clayey soil.
| Biotite Gneiss unit (bg) – Gray to grayish-brown to dark gray,\x20
| medium- to coarse-grained, commonly schistose, generally pegmati-
| tic (biotite-muscovite-quartz-potassium-feldspar pegmatites),\x20
| biotite-rich gneiss with generally rare but locally fairly common\x20
| layers, lenses, and pods of hornblende-plagioclase amphibolite.\x20
| Characteristically and commonly contains small pods and lenses of\x20
| altered meta-ultramafic rocks. The biotite gneiss weathers to a\x20
| uniform, slightly micaceous, dark-red saprolite and clayey dark red\x20
| soil; vermiculitic mica is characteristic of soils formed from the\x20
| biotite gneiss.
| Button Schist unit (bs) – Dark gray to brownish-gray, medium-\x20
| to coarse-grained, lustrous (where fresh), (± chlorite)-garnet-\x20
| biotite-muscovite-plagioclase-(± microcline)-quartz button schist\x20
| with tiny black opaques. In most outcrops the schist contains large\x20
| muscovite fish that weather to buttons. The button schist is resistant\x20
| to weathering.
| Diabase (dia) – Fine- to medium-grained, dark gray to black augite\x20
| diabase, in dikes generally 16 to 66 feet wide. The diabase weathers\x20
| to a dark red clayey soil containing speheroidal boulders with fresh\x20
| rock inside an armoring, ocherous rind.\x20
| Granite Gneiss unit (gg) – Complex of granite and granitic gneiss.\x20
| Light gray to whitish-gray, medium-grained muscovite-biotite-
| microcline-oligoclase-quartz gneiss having well defined gneissic\x20
| layering. Most commonly is poorly foliated. Pavement outcrops,\x20
| “whale-back” outcrops, and boulder outcrops are characteristic of\x20
| this granite gneiss. Where deeply weathered, the gneiss forms thin\x20
| light whitish-yellow sandy soils.
| Magnetite quartzite (mq) – Thinly layered (0.4 inch) to laminated,\x20
| medium-grained, magnetite quartzite in units about 1 to 20 feet\x20
| thick. Commonly has thin (from 0.4 to 1.6 inches) quartz-magnetite\x20
| layers, with magnetite crystals as much as 0.4 inch in size, but com-
| monly about 0.04 inch. The quartz-magnetite layers alternate with\x20
| quartz layers without magnetite, or quartz layers with a small per-
| centage of magnetite, from about 1.6 to 3.2 inches thick. Magnetite\x20
| clumps that generally disrupt the layering are locally as large as\x20
| 8 inches, but are commonly about 0.4 inch.
| Quartzite/Schist unit (qs) – White to yellowish, sugary, to vitreous,\x20
| slightly graphitic to nongraphitic quartzite with accessory musco-
| vite, garnet and aluminosilicate minerals (kyanite, staurolite, or silli-
| manite), in layers from about 1 to 4 feet thick, interlayered with\x20
| feldspathic quartzite and garnetiferous quartz-muscovite or musco-
| vite-quartz schist. The aluminous schist part of the unit is commonly\x20
| a tan to yellow weathering, sheared or button-textured, commonly\x20
| quartzose, garnet-biotite-plagioclase-muscovite-quartz schist that\x20
| generally contains kyanite or staurolite.Well-Naming System
| In this report, wells are named using a system based on USGS\x20
| 7½-minute topographic quadrangle maps. Each topographic map\x20
| in Georgia has been assigned a number and letter designation begin-
| ning at the southwestern corner of the State. Numbers increase east-
| ward through 39; letters advance northward through “Z,” then double-
| letter designations “AA” through “PP” are used. The letters “I,”\x20
| “O,” “II,” and “OO” are not used. Wells inventoried in each quad-
| rangle are named sequentially beginning with “1.” Thus, the 55th\x20
| well inventoried in the Lawrenceville 7.5 minute quadrangle (des-
| ignated 14FF) in Gwinnett County is designated as well 14FF55.
| Supplemental Data on CD–ROM
| Supplemental data on this CD–ROM include detailed lithol-
| ogy, fracture, yield, and water-level data collected at various wells\x20
| in the study area. These data are presented in a nonproprietary\x20
| Geographic Information System (GIS) database file format that\x20
| includes geographic and tabular data. A copy of ArcExplorer®, a\x20
| free GIS data viewer developed by Environmental Systems\x20
| Research Institute, is included on this CD–ROM for use with the\x20
| GIS database and allows a user to view the data spatially and query\x20
| the data of interest. In addition, much of the data are linked to a\x20
| hypertext markup language (HTML) document to allow a user to\x20
| access and view the data tables through text and Adobe® Portable\x20
| Document Format (PDF) files. A description of the GIS data and\x20
| figures contained on this CD–ROM is provided in the section\x20
| “Organization and Presentation of Hydrogeologic Data.”

| 4Reported air-lift yield from 8-inch well after reaming
| 5Five feet of slotted screen used below casingMethods of Data Collection \x20
| and Analysis
| A variety of methods were used to collect and analyze\x20
| data during this study. These methods consisted of indirect and\x20
| direct measurement of various hydrologic and geologic proper-
| ties by test-well drilling, geophysical logging and borehole-
| camera surveys, flowmeter surveys, aquifer testing, packer\x20
| testing, and water-level monitoring.
| Test-Well Drilling
| Test-well drilling was used to obtain detailed subsurface\x20
| information about rock types, fracture zone(s), rock fabrics, and\x20
| hydrologic characteristics of water-bearing zones. Many of the\x20
| wells were drilled using air-percussion rotary methods and con-
| structed as open-hole wells. Two existing wells, 14FF10 and\x20
| 14FF08, reportedly were drilled using the cable-tool method.\x20
| A list of wells, locations, and construction details is provided\x20
| in table 2. Well locations are shown in figures 1 and 2.
| Air-Percussion Rotary Drilling and \x20
| Well Construction
| Air-percussion rotary drilling was used for constructing test\x20
| wells during the study. This drilling technique, which uses a\x20
| down-hole air hammer, is the most suitable drilling method in\x20
| crystalline-rock formations and also is rapid and inexpensive.\x20
| The method uses air as a circulating medium to cool the air\x20
| hammer, bring drill cuttings to the surface, and maintain bore-
| hole integrity. When drilling, the cuttings are removed from the\x20
| borehole using high-pressure air. The air that is circulated also\x20
| cools the drill bit as it circulates from inside the drill rod and out\x20
| and around the bit. Water is added to the air stream when the\x20
| borehole does not produce sufficient amounts of water to carry\x20
| rock cuttings to land surface.
| Test wells were constructed by (1) advancing an 8- to \x20
| 12-inch-diameter borehole through unconsolidated soil,\x20
| saprolite and the upper portion of bedrock; (2) grouting a\x20
| polyvinyl chloride (PVC) surface casing to seal off the upper\x20
| zones; and (3) drilling a 6-inch-diameter borehole into the\x20
| underlying bedrock. When discussing fractures, yield, or\x20
| other physical features within the open portion of the test well\x20
| (below the surface casing) the term borehole is used hereafter.\x20
| The term well is used to describe the performance characteris-
| tics of water- bearing zones intersecting the entire length of\x20
| the test well.
| Field observations made at the time of drilling included the\x20
| depth, drilling rate, size of cuttings, changes in lithology, and\x20
| color of the drilling fluid. The rate of ground-water production\x20
| from the test well also was monitored to determine the depths of\x20
| water-bearing fractures.
| Lithologic Sampling and Determination of Rock Type
| Rock cuttings collected during drilling were used to deter-
| mine subsurface lithology and estimate the approximate depth\x20
| of lithologic contacts. Samples were collected in a wire-mesh\x20
| basket placed below the rotary table where water and cuttings\x20
| were returned to the land surface. The wire-mesh basket was\x20
| emptied every 10 ft that the hole was drilled, and the samples\x20
| were washed, cleaned, and dried on a preparation board. Rock\x20
| cuttings were then examined with a hand lens to determine\x20
| approximate mineral and rock-type percentages.
text: default page 16
| Methods of Data Collection and Analysis 9If the sample contained more than 50 percent of a specific\x20
| rock constituent, then the dominant component was listed first,\x20
| followed by the minor component (separated by the “w/” code,\x20
| which is short for “with”). For example, if a sample contained\x20
| between 60 and 80 percent amphibolite with the remainder\x20
| being biotite gneiss, the interval would be identified with the\x20
| code: a-w/bg. Likewise, a sample with predominantly biotite\x20
| gneiss and lesser amounts of amphibolite would be identified\x20
| with the code bg-w/a. Samples with approximate equal\x20
| amounts of different rock types were separated with a dash,\x20
| with the rock types not listed in any specific order of predomi-
| nance. A sample with equal amounts of amphibolite, biotite\x20
| gneiss, and button schist, for example, would be assigned the\x20
| code a-bg-bs. Samples with more than one minor rock type\x20
| component were identified following the dominant rock code\x20
| and the “w/.” For example, a sample with predominantly\x20
| amphibolite with some biotite-hornblende gneiss and biotite\x20
| gneiss would have the code a-w/bhg-bg.
| Using the lithologic unit definitions of Chapman and others\x20
| (1999), the rock types were grouped into one of the seven prin-
| cipal lithologic units (table 1). In general, this grouping was\x20
| based on the dominant rock type identified in the rock-cutting\x20
| samples. If the dominant rock type was amphibolite, for exam-
| ple, with layers and lenses of other rock types, it would be\x20
| assigned to the amphibolite unit. Some weight also was given to\x20
| the gamma log response; a distinctive gamma response in some\x20
| wells was associated with a specific rock unit, such as the button\x20
| schist unit, to define contacts. Most lithologic contacts, however,\x20
| were defined based on rock-cutting samples from the wells.
| Determining the precise depth of lithologic contacts was\x20
| complicated by cuttings sloughed off from shallower portions\x20
| of the test well and by having a 10-ft-long sampling interval. In\x20
| the absence of any definitive contact data, such as a distinct\x20
| color change of the return water, the depths of contacts were\x20
| located at the end of the 10-ft-long sampling interval. Two wells\x20
| (14FF26 and 14FF42) were cored, which is the most precise\x20
| method of defining the depths of lithologic contacts.
| Some wells did not have any rock cuttings from which to\x20
| produce lithologic logs. For these wells, geophysical logs were\x20
| used to correlate to the lithology observed in nearby wells.\x20
| Lithologic contacts in wells 14FF10, 14FF16, 14FF17, and 14FF18\x20
| (all located in the Rhodes Jordan Wellfield) were based on the\x20
| corehole at well 14FF26 and correlated using geophysical logs.\x20
| Similarly, the depths of lithologic contacts in 14FF08 and\x20
| 13FF12 were estimated by correlating to nearby well 14FF50.
| Well Development and Short-Term Yield
| Drilled, open-hole test wells do not require extensive clean-
| ing or development to prepare them for permanent use. The test\x20
| wells were developed at the end of drilling to clean the open\x20
| portion of the boreholes of drill cuttings and debris and to provide\x20
| a driller’s estimate of short-term yield (the short-term yield is\x20
| estimated by the well driller at the end of the drilling and/or\x20
| development process and is the reported discharge rate that can\x20
| be sustained from the well during a relatively short period of\x20
| time). After drilling to total depth in each well, the driller con-
| tinued to air lift and discharge water entering the well until the\x20
| return water was relatively clear and did not contain appreciable\x20
| sand or rock fragments. This development process was com-
| pleted quickly for low-yielding wells but took considerable\x20
| time (3 hours or more) for high-yield wells.
| Following well development the driller estimated the short-
| term yield. For most test wells, the water being discharged from\x20
| the well was conveyed along a ditch into a 55-gallon drum or a\x20
| calibrated container for measurement. The rate at which water\x20
| was air lifted out of the well was carefully monitored to determine\x20
| the maximum discharge that could be sustained (i.e., short-term\x20
| yield). Well drillers routinely measure short-term yield, and these\x20
| measurements are considered reliable indicators of yield com-
| pared to short-term aquifer tests (Paillet and Duncanson, 1994).
| In some test wells, the drilling rig was “drowned out” before\x20
| reaching the target drilling depth. Air-percussion rotary drilling\x20
| rigs are drowned out when ground water enters the well faster\x20
| than the drilling rig can air lift the water out of the well—typi-
| cally the result of a bottom-hole fracture. Hence, in drowned-
| out wells, the reported yield at the time of drowning typically is\x20
| less than the actual yield.
| Geophysical Logging and Borehole-Camera Surveys
| Wells in the Lawrenceville area were logged using various\x20
| geophysical tools and inspected with a submersible borehole\x20
| camera to aid in characterizing the lithology and identifying\x20
| water-bearing zones. Geophysical logs were collected in water-
| filled 6- to 10-inch-diameter open boreholes. Table 3 lists the\x20
| logging tools used, types of measurements made, and uses of\x20
| these data. A typical suite of logs from each well consisted of a\x20
| caliper log, natural-gamma log, resistivity logs (short normal,\x20
| long normal, and lateral), fluid resistivity and temperature logs,\x20
| and borehole televiewer image logs. Electromagnetic-conduc-
| tivity logs also were collected in 21 wells but were not used to\x20
| characterize lithology or identify water-bearing zones. Data\x20
| from the electromagnetic-conductivity logs, however, are\x20
| included on this CD–ROM. The types of logs collected in each\x20
| well are summarized in table 4.
| Caliper Logging
| Caliper logs were used to measure hole diameter and\x20
| identify open fractures, voids, and other distinct openings in the\x20
| bedrock. The caliper has three spring-mounted arms that\x20
| measure the average diameter of the borehole and, hence, is\x20
| used to identify zones of borehole enlargement typically\x20
| associated with bedrock fractures. Borehole enlargements also\x20
| occur in zones of weaker or more friable rock and, therefore,\x20
| were examined visually using a borehole televiewer and a\x20
| borehole camera to verify the character of the fracture.\x20
| Hairline4 “tight” joints generally could not be distinguished\x20
| using caliper logs.4In this report the term hairline is used to describe joints with little to no aperture or physical opening.
text: default page 17
| 10 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaTable 3. Description of borehole geophysical tools used in the Lawrenceville, Georgia, area.
| Geophysical tools Measures Uses
| Caliper Borehole diameter Identify breakouts and potential fractures
| Multiparameter log Natural gamma, 64-inch normal resistivity, 16-inch normal\x20
| resistivity, lateral resistivity, spontaneous potential, fluid\x20
| resistivity, fluid temperature
| Identify lithology, water-bearing zones, changes\x20
| in water chemistry and temperature
| Borehole acoustic televiewer Oriented image of borehole wall, borehole deviation Calculate orientation of subsurface features, cal-
| culate borehole deviation
| Electromagnetic flowmeter Fluid movement in borehole Identify zones of inflow and outflow from the\x20
| borehole during static or pumping conditions
| Heat Pulse Flowmeter Low-velocity fluid movement Identify flow directions in static or low-flow\x20
| pumping conditions
| Borehole Image Processing\x20
| System (manufactured by\x20
| RaaX Company Ltd.)
| High-resolution oriented optical images of borehole wall Calculate orientation of subsurface featuresNatural-Gamma Logging
| Natural-gamma logs were used to help characterize rock\x20
| types in wells. Chapman and others (1999) noted a characteris-
| tic lower baseline response (from 0 to 292 American Petroleum\x20
| Institute units [API units]) for the amphibolite unit compared to\x20
| the button schist and biotite gneiss (from 200 to 400 API units).
| Resistivity Logging
| Resistivity logs were used to detect potential water-bearing\x20
| fracture zones in the bedrock. Resistivity is a measure of the\x20
| bedrock formation’s electrical resistance (expressed in square\x20
| ohmmeters per meter [ohm/m]). Water-bearing fracture zones\x20
| in highly resistive igneous and metamorphic rocks commonly\x20
| are associated with a zone of decreased resistivity. Resistivities\x20
| of igneous and metamorphic rocks penetrated by wells in this\x20
| study area ranged from 1,000 to 4,000 ohm/m for unfractured\x20
| rock, and from 100 to 500 ohm/m for fractured rock having\x20
| water-bearing zones. Three types of resistivity logs were col-
| lected: (1) 16-inch normal (measures the formation resistivity\x20
| within a roughly 3-ft spherical zone around the borehole),\x20
| (2) 64-inch normal (measures the formation resistivity within a\x20
| 10-ft spherical zone or less), and (3) lateral (measures the resis-
| tivity of a small volume of bedrock material in the formation\x20
| without involving the material nearest to the borehole). Both\x20
| 16- and 64-inch normal resistivity logs are sensitive to varia-
| tions in borehole diameter. Because of the similar response of\x20
| these logs, only the lateral log is shown in figures in this report.
| Fluid-Temperature and Fluid-Resistivity Logging
| Fluid-temperature and fluid-resistivity logs were used to\x20
| detect changes in water temperature and water chemistry.\x20
| Changes in fluid temperature and resistivity usually indicate\x20
| inflow or outflow of water from the borehole; and, hence,\x20
| inflections on fluid temperature and resistivity logs can be used\x20
| to help identify water-bearing zones. The fluid temperature and\x20
| resistivity logs typically are used in combination with other logs\x20
| to verify a water-bearing zone at the inflection point.
| Borehole-Televiewer Imaging
| Two types of borehole-televiewer imaging techniques were\x20
| used during the study: acoustic televiewer (ATV) and optical\x20
| televiewer using the Borehole Image Processing System\x20
| (BIPS). The ATV uses sound waves to collect a magnetically\x20
| oriented (to magnetic north) image of the borehole wall. Fea-
| tures such as simple fractures, voids, foliation, and layering can\x20
| be identified in the acoustic image. Unlike the ATV, which uses\x20
| sound waves, the BIPS tool records an optical image of the\x20
| borehole wall. The BIPS log has a substantially higher resolu-
| tion and allows for more subtle features to be identified. The\x20
| BIPS log was used in the same manner as the ATV log.
| Borehole-Camera Surveys
| A borehole camera was used to visually inspect and docu-
| ment joints, fractures and other structures intersecting the bore-
| hole wall. A Geovision™ high-resolution camera was used to\x20
| collect the video images in most of the open boreholes of wells.\x20
| Because the Geovision™ camera has a fixed head, a survey first\x20
| was made with the camera head pointed downward and a sec-
| ond survey with the camera head horizontal. The downward\x20
| survey provided the best view of steeply-dipping5 fractures and\x20
| large open fractures intersecting the borehole wall. The hori-
| zontal survey allowed viewing of small-scale rock fabric, open\x20
| fractures, and other small voids and openings.5In this report, “steeply dipping” refers to angles typically greater than 70 degrees from horizontal.

text: default page 19
| 12 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaCharacterization of Fractures in Open Boreholes
| Imaging techniques used in this study permitted direct\x20
| observation of lithology, compositional layering, foliation, and\x20
| other structures in relation to the depth and nature of the inter-
| secting fracture plane. When viewing fractures, some interpre-
| tation was required to separate them into several main types.\x20
| Determination of Type, Depth, and\x20
| Orientation of Fractures
| ATV and BIPS logs, in combination with borehole-camera\x20
| surveys, were used to determine the type of fractures in open\x20
| boreholes. The strike and dip of intersecting fractures were\x20
| compared to surrounding foliation and compositional layering\x20
| and classified as “joints” where fractures crosscut rock foliation\x20
| and compositional layering, “open joints” for fractures with vis-
| ible openings that could be seen intersecting the borehole wall,\x20
| “foliation partings” for small openings formed parallel to folia-
| tion or compositional layering, and “major foliation openings”\x20
| for large openings formed parallel to foliation or compositional\x20
| layering. Irregular openings, for which a category was not\x20
| readily apparent, were classified into the primary feature being\x20
| weathered. For example, weathered and dissolutioned joints\x20
| were included in the open joint category and irregular openings\x20
| along foliation planes were included as a foliation parting or a\x20
| major foliation opening depending on the size of the fracture.
| Depths of fractures identified from the ATV and/or BIPS\x20
| logs were depth corrected to align vertically with the caliper and\x20
| other geophysical log data. In order to make the correction, the\x20
| caliper log and the image logs first were plotted on the same\x20
| scale, and then prominent fractures on the image logs were\x20
| matched to peaks on the caliper log. An average depth offset\x20
| was then computed to make the depth correction. For example,\x20
| if the caliper log indicated fracture openings at 10 ft, 100 ft, and\x20
| 245 ft that corresponded to fractures observed on the image log\x20
| at 11 ft, 101 ft, and 246 ft, then a depth correction of 1 ft was\x20
| used for the ATV or BIPS log. In this manner, the fractures and\x20
| other structural features identified with geophysical tools were\x20
| shifted to a common reference point—that being the caliper log,\x20
| which is referenced to land surface. Small offsets in vertical\x20
| alignment commonly occur because of different tool sizes and\x20
| shifts in the reference point used for logging.
| Overall, the caliper log, in combination with the borehole\x20
| ATV and/or BIPS logs, provided ample information to locate\x20
| most of the fractures in the open boreholes of wells. In many\x20
| wells, however, the borehole-camera log was used to aid in\x20
| identifying less obvious joints and fractures that were difficult\x20
| to see in the ATV or BIPS log. Correlating between the high-
| resolution camera log and the image logs was an effective\x20
| means for identifying less obvious intersecting joints and frac-
| tures. Small openings, such as foliation partings, rarely were\x20
| accompanied by an observable caliper peak; therefore, camera\x20
| logs were necessary for identifying these small features.
| The orientation (strike and dip) of fractures was determined\x20
| from the ATV and BIPS logs. The strike and dip indicate the\x20
| orientation of the layer or planar feature relative to a horizontal\x20
| plane. Strike is the compass direction of a line formed by the\x20
| intersection of the surface of an inclined feature with an imagi-
| nary horizontal plane (fig. 3). Dip is the tilt or angle, perpendic-
| ular to strike, of an inclined feature measured downward from a\x20
| horizontal plane. In figures included in this report, the structural\x20
| orientation of features is shown in terms of the dip angle (mea-
| sured from horizontal) and the dip azimuth (using 360-degree\x20
| compass direction) using a tadpole plot (fig. 3).\x20
| WellCad©, a commercial log processing program from\x20
| Advanced Logic Technology (ALT), was used to calculate the\x20
| strike and dip of structural features identified in televiewer logs.\x20
| When viewed in a two-dimensional projection of the borehole\x20
| wall, planar-dipping features form an ellipse across the borehole,\x20
| which appears as a sinusoidal wave on a two-dimensional depic-
| tion of the borehole wall (fig. 3). The lowest point on the sinu-
| soidal wave gives the direction of the dipping plane. The true\x20
| strike and dip were calculated using the WellCad© image-pro-
| cessing module, which corrects for borehole deviation (fig. 3).\x20
| A rotation of 3.5 degrees west of “true” north also was applied\x20
| to the data to correct for magnetic declination in the study area.\x20
| Identification of Water-Bearing Fractures
| Water-bearing fractures in open boreholes of wells were\x20
| identified by comparing the depth and yield of individual water-
| bearing zones identified during drilling to fracture data com-
| piled from geophysical logs, ATV and BIPS televiewer image\x20
| logs, borehole-camera images, and flowmeter logs. Water-bear-
| ing fractures were confirmed using multiple lines of evidence,\x20
| such as a caliper peak (indicating a physical opening), low-
| resistivity response on resistivity logs, and inflections on fluid\x20
| temperature and fluid resistivity logs. The geophysical log data\x20
| were used in combination with “visual” observations of the\x20
| fracture trace in the ATV or BIPS log, and borehole-camera log.\x20
| Using these sources of data, the main water-bearing fractures\x20
| were interpolated and denoted on geologic logs for each well. It\x20
| should be noted that the water-bearing zones identified in this\x20
| study represent primary water-bearing fractures in each well.\x20
| Joints and fractures not contributing substantially to the yield of\x20
| a well (much less than 1 gal/min) were not of concern unless the\x20
| well was low yielding.
| In some cases, water-bearing fractures were identified, but\x20
| the yield from individual fractures could not be quantified with\x20
| the available data. For example, in some wells the yield gained\x20
| along a vertical section of borehole could not be attributed to a\x20
| specific zone. In these vertical sections, the water-bearing zones\x20
| were called potential production zones to indicate the relative\x20
| uncertainty of the exact depth of the production zone. The term\x20
| potential production zone also was applied to the most likely\x20
| production zones in some of the older existing wells for which\x20
| drilling observations were not available.

text: default page 21
| 14 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaEstimating Yield Contribution from Individual \x20
| Water-Bearing Fractures
| The yield contribution from individual water-bearing frac-
| tures was estimated from air-lift yield tests at the time of drilling\x20
| and then confirmed later with flowmeter surveys. The combina-
| tion of these two methods provided the most effective means of\x20
| characterizing the yield contribution from individual water-
| bearing fractures intersecting the open boreholes of wells.
| Estimating Yield during Drilling
| The depth and yield of fractures were initially estimated\x20
| during drilling by carefully observing the drilling rate, identify-
| ing “drilling breaks,” and measuring the amount of water being\x20
| evacuated from the test well during air-percussion rotary drill-
| ing. A drilling break occurs when the down-hole air hammer\x20
| penetrates an open void, a zone of increased fracturing, or\x20
| weakness in the rock. An increase in “chattering” of the drill\x20
| rod, followed by a distinct drop, and an almost immediate\x20
| increase in the return volume were indicative of large, open,\x20
| water-bearing fractures. Small openings and/or low-yielding\x20
| fractures typically corresponded with small drilling breaks,\x20
| chattering, and small rod drops, but without an increase in\x20
| return volume.
| The color and size of the rock cuttings also were used as an\x20
| indicator of potential water-bearing fracture zones. Water-bear-
| ing fracture zones commonly are accompanied by an increase in\x20
| the size of the drill cuttings, iron-oxide staining on the cuttings,\x20
| and large angular rock fragments broken out of fracture zones.
| The air-lift yield was checked after each potential water-
| bearing zone was penetrated to determine any increase in yield\x20
| from the zone. The yield measurement generally was made by\x20
| conveying the return water into a bucket or 55-gallon drum and\x20
| using a stopwatch to compute the discharge rate. At well sites\x20
| where there was no practical means to convey the water into a\x20
| bucket, the air-lift yield was estimated by the driller. After mak-
| ing the measurement, the increase in air-lift yield was attributed\x20
| to the zone penetrated. For example, if the well was blowing\x20
| “dry” down to 150 ft and “chattering” and a rod drop was\x20
| observed at 151 ft, followed by an increase in yield of 5 gal/min,\x20
| then a 5-gal/min water-bearing fracture was noted at a depth of\x20
| 151 ft. If another zone was penetrated at 200 ft, with a total dis-
| charge of 20 gal/min (increase of 15 gal/min), then a 15-gal/min\x20
| fracture zone was noted at 200 ft. This process was repeated for\x20
| all potential water-bearing fracture zones in the well.
| The yield contribution of individual fractures and fracture\x20
| zones was later estimated by carefully correlating air-lift yield\x20
| increases observed during drilling (described above) with the\x20
| depth of fracture zones identified using geophysical logging. In\x20
| some zones, the correlation was clear and unambiguous—an\x20
| increase in air-lift yield, for example, may have been observed\x20
| within 5 ft of a large fracture opening or fracture zone observed\x20
| in an ATV log. In other zones, increases in yield could not be\x20
| correlated with a specific fracture observed in the borehole geo-
| physical logs. In these cases, the increase in air-lift yield was\x20
| attributed to one or more previously penetrated (shallow) frac-
| ture zones near the depth of yield increase. A shallow fracture\x20
| zone, within 15–25 ft of the increase, often was identified as the\x20
| most likely water-bearing fracture zone. The yield estimated in\x20
| this manner should be regarded only as a rough estimate of the\x20
| yield from the fracture or fracture zone. Limitations of this\x20
| method are: (1) accurately measuring the amount of water being\x20
| lifted out of the well during drilling—some were measured and\x20
| others estimated; (2) identifying the precise location of slight\x20
| increases—increases of 1 to 10 gal/min may not be recognized\x20
| until starting a new segment of drilling rod; and (3) detecting\x20
| small increases in yield below one or two major producing\x20
| zones—for example, an increase from 1 to 5 gal/min may be\x20
| indistinguishable from a shallow (previously penetrated) frac-
| ture zone producing 100 gal/min.
| Flowmeter Surveys
| Flowmeter surveys, conducted under ambient and pumping\x20
| conditions, were used to identify water-producing and water-
| losing fracture zones in open-bedrock wells. Water-producing\x20
| zones are defined in this report as discrete zones that yield mea-
| surable amounts of water to the borehole when pumped or\x20
| where water is flowing into the borehole during ambient condi-
| tions. Water-losing zones are defined as zones where water\x20
| exits the well through fractures or zones of lower hydraulic head.
| The technique of locating water-producing and water-losing\x20
| zones involves using a flowmeter to measure the flow velocity\x20
| (up or down) along segments of open borehole or at discrete\x20
| depths, such as above or below previously identified fractures.\x20
| Data are obtained by either measuring flow at a stationary point\x20
| or by trolling along sections of a borehole to produce a flow pro-
| file (Johnson and Williams, 2003). Differences in flow rate\x20
| detected with the flowmeter along the borehole are attributed to\x20
| water-producing or water-losing zones. For this study, the sta-
| tionary-point method was found to be the most reliable method\x20
| for measurement of borehole flow, and those are the data reported.
| Two types of flowmeters were used for flow measurement:\x20
| a heat-pulse flowmeter (HPFM) and an electromagnetic (EM)\x20
| flowmeter. The HPFM can measure flow rates as small as\x20
| 0.01 gal/min and was used for wells having a borehole flow of\x20
| less than 2 gal/min. The EM flowmeter has a larger operating\x20
| range and was used for wells having a borehole flow of greater\x20
| than 2 gal/min.
| With the exception of well 14FF59, two separate flowmeter\x20
| surveys were conducted in each well: (1) a nonpumping “ambi-
| ent” survey and (2) a pumping survey. In each well, an ambient\x20
| survey was first conducted to document the ambient flow into\x20
| or out of the borehole prior to pumping. These surveys provided\x20
| information on whether there was any ambient flow resulting\x20
| from differences in head and hydraulic conductivity of the\x20
| water-bearing fractures intersecting the well. The rate of flow\x20
| from higher head fractures to lower head fractures is largely\x20
| controlled by the hydraulic conductivity of the fracture and by\x20
| differences in hydraulic head between fractures. Following the\x20
| ambient survey, a submersible pump was installed near the top\x20
text: default page 22
| Methods and Data Collection and Analysis 15of the well and the well was pumped at rates ranging from sev-
| eral gallons per minute to as much as 60 gal/min. After the\x20
| drawdown in the well had stabilized, a second flowmeter survey\x20
| was completed, measuring the flow at similar depths recorded\x20
| during the ambient survey. The results of the ambient and\x20
| pumping surveys then were plotted to show the relative contri-
| bution of water-bearing fractures with depth. An interpretive\x20
| line was drawn through the data points to produce a best fit for\x20
| the data, stepping the graph at interpreted inflow or outflow\x20
| points. The depths of water-bearing zones determined from geo-
| physical logs were taken into consideration in determining\x20
| where to designate the inflow or outflow for each log.
| Aquifer Testing
| The City of Lawrenceville, or its contractor, conducted\x20
| aquifer tests to determine pumping drawdown and yield for\x20
| water-production engineering. Because the goal of these tests\x20
| was for engineering purposes without determination of hydrau-
| lic properties, typical aquifer-test procedures and protocols\x20
| were not used. Close attention was paid, however, to the draw-
| down in the pumped well, pumping rate, and drawdown in\x20
| observation wells.
| For the aquifer tests, pressure transducers and water-level\x20
| data recorders were installed in both the pumped well and\x20
| nearby observation wells. These water-level recording devices\x20
| were left in place for a period of time ranging from a few days\x20
| to several weeks, depending on the amount of time required for\x20
| recovery of the water level after pumping. Barometric pressure\x20
| and rainfall gages were used during some of the tests so that\x20
| barometric changes and rainfall recharge effects could be\x20
| accounted for, where necessary. Discharge was regulated using\x20
| gate valves and measured with a flowmeter or an orifice weir.\x20
| The discharge from the aquifer tests was conveyed away from\x20
| the pumped well by routing the flow along a ditch or piped to\x20
| the nearest stream or water body. The discharge rate was\x20
| checked by taking a volumetric measurement using a stopwatch\x20
| and a bucket. These “bucket checks” routinely were taken sev-
| eral times during the test for quality-assurance purposes.
| To avoid dewatering fractures, most of the aquifer tests were\x20
| conducted by pumping at a rate large enough to draw the water\x20
| level in the pumped well down to a few feet above the upper-
| most water-bearing zone and holding the level above that zone\x20
| for the remainder of the test. As each test proceeded, the pump-
| ing rate was decreased, if necessary, to maintain the pumping\x20
| water level above the uppermost water-producing fracture zone.\x20
| A small-diameter stilling well (typically 1 inch) was used to\x20
| make manual water-level measurements in the pumped well.\x20
| The stilling well prevented cascading water in the pumped well\x20
| from interfering with water-level measurements.
| Analysis of the data consisted of plotting the drawdown in\x20
| the pumped well with respect to time and evaluating if the\x20
| pumping water level stabilized. From these observations, the\x20
| pumping rate and pumping water level were determined. Draw-
| down in observation wells also was evaluated to see if any\x20
| anisotropic response could be identified. Quantitative analysis\x20
| for determination of aquifer coefficients using the drawdown\x20
| and recovery data is beyond the scope of this report.
| Packer Testing
| Inflatable straddle packers were used to isolate and\x20
| hydraulically test individual discrete fractures in well 14FF59\x20
| (figs. 1, 4). To conduct each test, the packers were inflated to\x20
| isolate a section of borehole, water was pumped from the iso-
| lated section, and pressure transducers were used to monitor the\x20
| hydraulic response within, above, and below the pumped zone.
| A special packer system (fig. 4) was designed to conduct\x20
| packer tests in the Lawrenceville area. The packer system con-
| sisted of two inflatable rubber packers, a submersible pump, and\x20
| pressure transducers for monitoring above and below the pack-
| ers (top and bottom transducers) and the isolated interval (mid-
| dle transducer). Pass-through tubes connect pressure transduc-
| ers to monitoring intervals. Because the pass-through tubes are\x20
| open ended and trap air when lowered into the borehole, an air-
| actuated valve was used to evacuate trapped air in the pass-
| through tubes. The rubber packers were inflated using nitrogen\x20
| delivered through a ¼-inch polyethylene air line. The pressure\x20
| in the packers was monitored using a pressure gage. The pump-
| ing rate was monitored at the land surface with a flowmeter and\x20
| checked with a calibrated bucket and stopwatch.
| Following inflation of packers and stabilization of the water\x20
| levels in the three zones, the submersible pump was used to\x20
| pump water from the isolated interval. Each test continued until\x20
| the water level in the pumped interval was static or nearly static.\x20
| Following the pumping phase of the test, the pump was stopped\x20
| and water-level recovery was monitored.
| The data were analyzed by plotting the water-level response\x20
| in the three zones with respect to time. This allowed a qualita-
| tive analysis of the data to determine the hydraulic response\x20
| within and between discrete water-bearing fractures.
| Water-Level Monitoring
| Water levels were measured synoptically, where many wells\x20
| were measured during a short period of time, and continuously\x20
| to monitor the water-level fluctuation in response to seasonal\x20
| variations in rainfall and pumping.
| Synoptic water levels were measured in 30 wells throughout\x20
| the study area on October 31, 2001. An electronic water-level\x20
| tape was used to measure the depth of water below land surface\x20
| and a pressure transducer was used to measure water levels in\x20
| flowing wells. Water levels above land surface are expressed as\x20
| negative numbers. Continuous water levels were measured\x20
| using data recorders installed in 26 wells for periods of time\x20
| ranging from more than 1 month to several years. Water-level\x20
| recorders were installed and operated using standard procedures\x20
| of the USGS. All data were entered into the USGS National\x20
| Water Information System database.

| Hydrogeologic Data 17Hydrogeologic Data
| Thirty-two bedrock wells, ranging in depth from 180 to\x20
| 630 ft, were used to characterize the lithology, yield, and frac-
| ture characteristics in the Lawrenceville area. In addition, sev-
| eral shallow regolith wells were used as observation wells dur-
| ing aquifer tests and for long-term monitoring. For each well,\x20
| the latitude, longitude, land-surface altitude, top of casing alti-
| tude, well depth, casing depth, casing diameter and type, ream\x20
| depth, well yield, and geologic units are listed in table 2. Well\x20
| locations are shown in figures 1 and 2.
| Fracture Data
| Fracture data, compiled from geophysical and borehole-
| camera logs in 32 bedrock wells, were used to determine the\x20
| depth, nature, and yield of bedrock fractures in the Lawrence-
| ville area. These data were plotted on standard figures for each\x20
| well as illustrated in figures 5 and 6. Typically, two or three\x20
| main water-bearing fractures were detected in each well during\x20
| drilling. Although geophysical surveys indicated numerous\x20
| fractures in each well, flowmeter surveys and packer tests iden-
| tified only a few of these as water bearing—typically the larger\x20
| “open” fractures intersecting boreholes.
| Using borehole imaging data and correlating these data to\x20
| individual fracture yield, two main systems of water-bearing\x20
| fractures were identified: (1) joints, open joints, and zones of\x20
| joint concentration consisting mostly of steeply-dipping low-
| yielding fractures; and (2) small and large openings along foli-
| ation planes and layering consisting of discontinuous but high-
| yield fractures, which are defined in this report as “foliation par-
| allel-parting systems” and originally described by Williams\x20
| (2003) as “foliation fracture systems.” Water-bearing zones\x20
| also are formed from the dissolution of preexisting crosscutting\x20
| mineralized joints, which form irregular openings that cross\x20
| foliation and layering. Chemical dissolution along joints, folia-
| tion planes, and contacts between compositional layers also\x20
| form a variety of irregular-shaped voids and irregular fractures.
| Individual Fracture Yield
| Yield of individual water-bearing fractures and fracture\x20
| zones was estimated by carefully correlating air-lift yield increases\x20
| observed during drilling with identified subsurface fractures.\x20
| Yields determined for individual fractures and fracture zones in\x20
| 32 wells, ranged from less than 1 gal/min to 240 gal/min (table 5).
| In general, increases in yield during drilling were found to\x20
| be associated with one or more of the following features:
| • joints, open joints, and zones of joint concentration;
| • foliation partings and major foliation openings along\x20
| foliation and layering of the rock;\x20
| • dissolution openings of mineral infilling of a joint\x20
| or vein; and\x20
| • irregular-shaped voids and fractures.
| Joints, Open Joints, and Zones of Joint Concentration
| Joints generally are complex “crisscrossing” fractures that\x20
| break up the rock into blocks and slabs of differing dimensions.\x20
| In boreholes, joints are differentiated from other fractures by\x20
| their occurrence in sets, common planar geometry, and orienta-
| tion cutting across rock foliation and layering. Most joints\x20
| observed in boreholes appeared as hairline fractures and typi-
| cally were not significant water-bearing zones.
| Open joints and zones of joint concentration typically were\x20
| associated with increases in air-lift yield from 1 to 5 gal/min,\x20
| although slightly higher or lower yields are common for these\x20
| types of fractures. Several wells intercept zones of intensely\x20
| jointed rock. One such well, 14FF58, penetrates multiple zones\x20
| of joint concentration. Despite these many zones of joint con-
| centration, however, only three open joints were identified for a\x20
| combined yield of about 1 gal/min. In other wells, high yields\x20
| were obtained from zones of joint concentration and open\x20
| joints: the highest yielding zone was found in well 13FF22,\x20
| where a yield of about 30 gal/min was reported for a zone of\x20
| concentrated jointing and multiple open joints (table 5). This\x20
| zone of concentrated jointing showed extensive dissolution\x20
| around the joint faces, which apparently has enhanced the yield\x20
| from this specific zone. Other wells producing water from\x20
| joints, open joints, or zones of joint concentrations include\x20
| wells 13FF15, 13FF23, and 14FF17.
| Foliation Partings and Major Foliation Openings
| Foliation partings and major foliation openings are fractures\x20
| formed nearly parallel to foliation and compositional layering;\x20
| thus, they occur only in layered metamorphic rock sequences\x20
| such as the amphibolite, biotite gneiss, button schist and quartz-
| ite/schist units. A foliation parting is defined in this report as a\x20
| small fracture with an aperture typically less than 0.5 inch. A\x20
| major foliation opening is a much larger fracture with an aper-
| ture typically ranging from 1 to 8 inches, with some larger.
| Individual foliation partings were associated with increases\x20
| in air-lift yield from 1 to 15 gal/min (table 5). In some wells,\x20
| groups of foliation partings or single partings yielded substan-
| tial amounts of water. A zone of water-bearing foliation part-
| ings in well 14FF52, from a depth of 158 to 159 ft, yielded\x20
| 30 gal/min; and a single foliation parting in well 13FF23, from\x20
| a depth of 82.5 to 83.5 ft, yielded 33 gal/min. In another well,\x20
| 14FF27, a yield of 50 gal/min was estimated for a group of foli-
| ation partings and one open joint in a zone between 410 and\x20
| 435 ft (table 5).
| Major foliation openings yielded substantially more water\x20
| than the smaller foliation partings described above. A range in\x20
| yield from 50 to 100 gal/min is typical for these larger fracture\x20
| openings (table 5), making these the primary water-bearing fea-
| tures responsible for high-yield wells in the Lawrenceville area.\x20
| All the high-yield wells studied in the Lawrenceville area pen-
| etrate at least one major foliation opening. In some wells, such\x20
| as wells 13FF16 and 13FF21, only one major foliation opening\x20
| was penetrated; whereas, in other wells such as well 13FF23,\x20
| multiple foliation openings were penetrated.

| Hydrogeologic Data 23Dissolution Openings
| Mineral-filled joints, veins, and possibly other features, such\x20
| as dikes and sills of pegmatite or aplite, appear be susceptible to\x20
| chemical dissolution, depending on the mineralogy. In several\x20
| wells, dissolution features along mineral-filled joints or veins\x20
| were associated with increases in air-lift yield up to 35 gal/min\x20
| (table 5). This amount exceeds the typical range in yield for open\x20
| joints and zones of joint concentration described above, indicat-
| ing the potential importance of dissolution features in the bed-
| rock. One such feature, at well 14FF53 at a depth from 298 to\x20
| 301 ft, yielded 35 gal/min from a single opening (table 5). These\x20
| features also were observed in well 14FF26 at a depth of 93-94 ft\x20
| and in well 13FF14 at a depth of 231–232 ft (table 5). It should\x20
| be noted that dissolution features may be more common than\x20
| indicated by the data because their identification requires a clear,\x20
| high-quality image that shows remnants of the mineral infilling\x20
| around the edges of the joint or vein. These types of high-quality\x20
| images were not available in most of the wells studied.
| Irregular-Shaped Voids and Fractures
| Under close examination, irregular-shaped voids and frac-
| tures appear to originate as joints, foliation partings/openings,\x20
| or a combination of different types of fracture openings that are\x20
| widened through chemical weathering and dissolution. These\x20
| features indicate that chemical weathering and dissolution prob-
| ably are important processes in widening and enhancement of\x20
| water-bearing fractures in the Lawrenceville area. One example\x20
| is from a zone of steeply-dipping joints in well 13FF22 at a\x20
| depth from 468 to 475 ft. In this zone, extensive dissolution\x20
| around steeply-dipping joints is evident, creating irregular\x20
| voids around the joints (see well-data summary for well 13FF22\x20
| on CD–ROM). Drusy crystalline cement also was observed in\x20
| this zone, indicating physical openings in the bedrock. Many of\x20
| the foliation fractures measured in boreholes also showed\x20
| rounded and irregular forms indicating substantial dissolution\x20
| of rock materials around the fracture face.
| Flowmeter Surveys
| Flowmeter surveys were used to confirm the yield from var-
| ious types of fractures intersecting open boreholes (table 6). In\x20
| several of the wells—including 13FF23, 14FF55, and 14FF59—\x20
| these surveys were used to identify confined/semiconfined\x20
| water-bearing zones. Water from the confined/semiconfined\x20
| zones flowed from the fracture, flowed up the borehole, and\x20
| typically exited from the borehole through shallow fractures.Table 6. Observations from ambient and pumping flowmeter surveys, Lawrenceville, Georgia, area.\x20

text: default page 31
| 24 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaWell 13FF23 is one example where water from confined/
| semiconfined water-bearing zones flows up the borehole and\x20
| exits out into shallow fractures. In this well, multiple water-
| bearing zones were detected between 100 and 250 ft using a\x20
| flowmeter (fig. 7). Under ambient conditions, water flowed\x20
| from fractures with high hydraulic head into the borehole, up\x20
| the borehole, and exited through fractures with lower head\x20
| below the bottom of the casing (fig. 7a). During pumping con-
| ditions, using a pumping rate of 50 gal/min, water entered the\x20
| borehole at water-bearing fractures with high head and flowed\x20
| up the borehole; however, the pumping did not reverse the\x20
| ambient flow regime, and water continued to exit the borehole\x20
| into fractures near the base of casing (fig. 7b, top arrows).\x20
| Although beyond the scope of this report, quantitative tech-
| niques could be used to infer the interval transmissivity and\x20
| interval hydraulic head (Paillet, 2000) from these data.
| Overall, flowmeter data indicate that ground water, under\x20
| ambient and pumping conditions, appears to be derived from\x20
| discrete fractures intersecting the boreholes, rather than evenly\x20
| distributed along open sections of the boreholes (table 6). Most\x20
| of the inflow typically was concentrated within two or three\x20
| intervals, corresponding to the depth of water-bearing fractures\x20
| identified through other geophysical methods.
| Aquifer-Test Data
| Aquifer tests were used to determine the long-term yield\x20
| from the wells, which is the yield of all water-bearing fractures\x20
| contributing water to the borehole during the period of the test.\x20
| Data from the aquifer tests also provided information on the\x20
| direction and magnitude of drawdown resulting from pumping,\x20
| which is an indicator of the contributing areas to the pumped\x20
| wells, and potential interference among wells.Figure 7. (A ) Flowmeter logs for well 13FF23, showing inflow and outflow from borehole, Lawrenceville,\x20
| Georgia. Top left-facing arrows indicate fractures where water is flowing out of the borehole. Right-facing\x20
| arrows show water entering borehole along factures between 100 and 250 feet. (B ) Caliper log shown for\x20
| reference. Flowmeter survey conducted on December 4, 2001.

| Hydrogeologic Data 25Aquifer-Test Yield, Drawdown, and Recovery
| Yields from aquifer tests ranged from 75 gal/min for well\x20
| 13FF16 to 600 gal/min for well 14FF50 for the period of the test\x20
| (table 7). In several wells, such as 13FF19 and 14FF55, the\x20
| aquifer-test yield was substantially less than the reported air-lift\x20
| yield (table 2). Well 13FF19 had an air-lift yield of 350-400gal/min\x20
| compared to an aquifer-test yield of 177 gal/min with about\x20
| 141 ft of drawdown. Well 14FF55 had an air-lift yield of\x20
| 325 gal/min compared to an aquifer-test yield of about \x20
| 241 gal/min with about 92 ft of drawdown. On the other hand,\x20
| well 13FF21, with an air-lift yield of 125 gal/min was only\x20
| 18 gal/min less than the yield of 107 gal/min determined from\x20
| the aquifer test. In other wells, the aquifer-test yield was greater\x20
| than the air-lift yield; well 14FF50 had an air-lift yield of\x20
| 300 gal/min, which is half of the aquifer-test yield of 600 gal/min.
| Collectively, data from the aquifer tests indicate that the\x20
| pumped wells were able to sustain large pumping rates6 for the\x20
| period of the tests. The recovery of water levels following\x20
| pumping varied from slow, indicating a small recharge rate to\x20
| the fracture(s) supplying the well, to fast, indicating a large\x20
| recharge rate to the fracture(s) supplying the well. One aquifer\x20
| test, indicating slow recovery, was conducted in well 13FF23.\x20
| During that test, a pumping rate of 449 gal/min was used for the\x20
| first 53 hours of the 72-hour test, causing a drawdown of about\x20
| 65 ft, approximately 18 ft above the uppermost water-bearing\x20
| fracture. For the remainder of the test, the pumping rate was\x20
| reduced to 342 gal/min to maintain the pumping water level at\x20
| about 65 ft. Although the water level stabilized at 342 gal/min,\x20
| the slow recovery following the end of the test indicated a small\x20
| recharge rate supplying the well. Another well showing slow\x20
| recovery was 13FF15, a 605-ft-deep well that was reamed to\x20
| 250 ft and had about 144 ft of available drawdown to the shal-
| lowest water-bearing fracture. The first aquifer test conducted\x20
| in this well used pumping rates of 210 gal/min for the first\x20
| 24 hours, from 240 to 290 gal/min for the next 24 hours, and\x20
| from 175 to 184 gal/min for the remainder of the 96-hour test.\x20
| Although the water level was nearly stable at the end of the test,\x20
| it took more than 10 days for the water level to recover 143 ft,\x20
| which was still about 8.5 ft lower than the initial static water\x20
| level. Water levels during a second aquifer test conducted in\x20
| well 13FF15, with a pumping rate of 200 gal/min for 48 hours,\x20
| never stabilized and required a similar amount of time to\x20
| recover as that observed in the first test.
| In contrast to the wells showing slow recovery, water levels\x20
| in several other wells recovered rapidly after pumping. Well\x20
| 13FF21 was pumped at a rate of about 107 gal/min for 72 hours\x20
| and recovered to 99 percent of the prepumping level in about\x20
| 35 hours. In another aquifer test, well 14FF59 was pumped for\x20
| 72 hours using a pumping rate ranging between 301 and\x20
| 444 gal/min, and the water level recovered in about 6 hours.\x20
| The rapid recovery in these two wells indicates a large recharge\x20
| rate into the bedrock fracture system supplying these wells in\x20
| comparison to other wells in the area.Table 7. Drawdown, pumping rate, and specific capacity of wells during aquifer tests in the Lawrenceville, Georgia, area.\x20
