| 26 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaDirection and Magnitude of Drawdown
| Ground-water level data collected from observation wells\x20
| during the aquifer tests were used to determine the direction and\x20
| magnitude of drawdown and to provide a general indication of\x20
| potential interference among production wells. The drawdown\x20
| in the observation wells varied depending on the distance and\x20
| direction from the pumped well.
| One aquifer test was conducted in well 13FF21 located on\x20
| the floodplain of the Yellow River (fig. 1). During that test, the\x20
| greatest drawdown occurred in a northwest-southeast direction\x20
| from the pumped well. A drawdown of 0.75 ft was observed in\x20
| well 13FF19, located about 6,800 ft from the pumped well and\x20
| parallel to the strike of lithologic units. No drawdown was\x20
| observed in well 13FF22 located about 5,400 ft away and per-
| pendicular to the strike of lithologic units. No drawdown was\x20
| observed in a shallow regolith well located 8 ft from the pumped\x20
| well, indicating no apparent hydrologic connection between the\x20
| bedrock fracture system and the regolith at this location.
| In an aquifer test conducted in well 13FF18, located on the\x20
| floodplain of Redland Creek (fig. 1), the greatest drawdown\x20
| occurred in an east-west direction from the pumped well. A\x20
| drawdown of 1.4 ft was observed in well 13FF17, located about\x20
| 2,800 ft west of the pumped well, and a drawdown greater than\x20
| 22.5 ft was observed in well 13FF13, located about 1,900 ft east\x20
| of the pumped well. The pumped well and these two observa-
| tion wells are aligned parallel to the strike of lithologic units and\x20
| penetrate the same lithologic units. No drawdown was observed\x20
| in well 13FF16, located about 2,500 ft north of the pumped well\x20
| and positioned across the strike of lithologic units.
| Data collected during aquifer tests in several other high-
| yield production wells — including 13FF23, 13FF19, and\x20
| 14FF50 — indicate similar drawdown to that described above.\x20
| In most of these tests, drawdown was greater in the direction of\x20
| bedrock foliation and layering than perpendicular to it (see table 7\x20
| for data from aquifer tests). As a result of this apparent prefer-
| ential drawdown, pumping interferences probably will occur\x20
| between certain wells. The largest potential interferences are\x20
| among wells 13FF21, 13FF23, and 13FF19 (fig. 1). The inter-
| ferences observed during the aquifer tests indicate that these\x20
| particular wells, if pumped simultaneously, likely would result\x20
| in corresponding declines in the pumping rates during long peri-
| ods of time. Smaller interferences also are evident between pro-
| duction wells at Rhodes Jordan Wellfield (14FF10 and 14FF16)\x20
| and wells 14FF08, 14FF50, 13FF15, 13FF16, and 14FF55.
| Packer-Test Data
| Packer-test data were used to determine the hydraulic\x20
| response among individual high-yield fractures at well 14FF59.\x20
| Using flowmeter data, six intervals (numbered 1 through 6),\x20
| each straddling the main water-bearing fractures in the well,\x20
| were selected for packer testing. Fracture depths, isolated bore-
| hole intervals, and the responses observed during the tests are\x20
| listed in table 8 and illustrated on sets of hydrographs included\x20
| on this CD–ROM. Each set of hydrographs, representing a sin-
| gle packer test, shows the pressure response recorded in the\x20
| interval above the packer (top transducer), below the packer\x20
| (bottom transducer) and in the test interval (middle transducer).
| The results of packer tests at well 14FF59 indicate\x20
| the following:
| • Shut-in pressure readings (head) taken before pumping\x20
| each of the packer intervals were above land surface and\x20
| substantially higher than the head in shallow fractures,\x20
| indicating that the water-bearing fractures in this well\x20
| are confined or semiconfined.
| • Pumping from test intervals 2 and 3 indicates that frac-
| tures at 267 and 282 ft are hydraulically connected as\x20
| indicated by the drawdown response in the bottom trans-
| ducer of the interval 2 test and the smaller drawdown\x20
| response in the top transducer of the interval 3 test. Con-
| versely, packer tests on intervals 4, 5, and 6 did not show\x20
| a substantial response in the top and bottom transducers,\x20
| indicating that hydraulic separation exists among these\x20
| fractures and the shallower fractures in the well.
| • Packer-test intervals 1 and 5 indicate low-permeability\x20
| fractures are present in these intervals. Low permeabil-
| ity is indicated by the pressure increase on the middle\x20
| transducer during packer inflation.
| The hydrologic responses observed at well 14FF59 indicate\x20
| that most of the water enters the borehole through foliation open-
| ings and not through joints, and confirms the high-yield nature of\x20
| major foliation openings in the well. A small amount of water\x20
| probably enters through joints, but this is much less than that\x20
| derived from the major foliation openings tested. Drawdown\x20
| responses from the packer tests also indicate a slight but notice-
| able vertical connection among individual high-yield foliation\x20
| openings, which likely is attributed to steeply-dipping joints.
| Water-Level Data
| Ground-water levels in the study area fluctuate in response\x20
| to seasonal variations in rainfall, stream stage, and ground-
| water pumping. Synoptic water-level data (table 9) were used to\x20
| provide information on the variation of water levels across the\x20
| study area. Continuous water-level data (table 10) were used to\x20
| monitor seasonal water-level fluctuations in response to rainfall\x20
| and to monitor drawdown in response to ground-water pump-
| ing. Water-level hydrographs are included on this CD–ROM for\x20
| each well where continuous data were available (table 10).
| Open-hole water levels7 in bedrock wells varied widely\x20
| across the area ranging from about 10 ft above land surface at\x20
| well 14FF59 to 54 ft below land surface at well 13FF15 (table 9).\x20
| Six boreholes had water levels above land surface and four of\x20
| these flowed when left uncapped. Most of the open-hole water\x20
| levels were above the bottom of casing, indicating confined/
| semiconfined conditions in the bedrock fracture system.7An open-hole water level represents a “composite” potentiometric head formed \x20
| by differing heads of individual fractures intersecting the open section of borehole.

| Seasonal ground-water level fluctuations varied from\x20
| about 1 to 3 ft for the longer periods of record and varied from\x20
| 0.25 to 0.5 foot during shorter periods (see hydrographs for\x20
| wells 13FF13 and 14FF47, CD–ROM) in response to variations\x20
| in rainfall. In these wells, ground-water levels generally\x20
| increased during wet periods in response to recharge from infil-
| trating precipitation and decreased during dry periods in\x20
| response to ground-water discharge to streams and evapotrans-
| piration. The magnitude of seasonal water-level fluctuations\x20
| observed in these wells is similar to longer trends observed in\x20
| other parts of the Piedmont region (Coffin and others, 2003).\x20
| Note that a large fluctuation of about 9 ft was observed in well\x20
| 14FF42 from June–November 1996; however, this magnitude\x20
| of fluctuation seems to be atypical for the Lawrenceville area.
| Variations in stream stage also may influence the water\x20
| level in at least one well. The water level in well 13FF22\x20
| increased approximately 1 ft in response to a rise in stream stage\x20
| (see hydrograph, CD–ROM).
| Water levels in about one-half of the wells used in this study\x20
| fluctuate in response to pumping at the Rhodes Jordan Well\x20
| Field (table 10). Some of the responses observed are summa-
| rized as follows:
| • In the immediate vicinity around the wellfield (wells\x20
| 14FF17, 14FF18, 14FF26, and 14FF39) drawdown in\x20
| response to pumping ranges between 75 and 135 feet,\x20
| depending on the rate and duration of pumping. Lower-
| ing the water level in the vicinity of Rhodes Jordan\x20
| Wellfield dewaters the shallow water-bearing fractures.\x20
| Pumping does not, however, lower the water level in a\x20
| nearby regolith observation wells (well 14FF36), indi-
| cating poor hydraulic connection between the bedrock\x20
| and regolith at this location.
| • Away from the wellfield, drawdown in the bedrock is\x20
| extensive and observed in wells as far away as 7,000 ft\x20
| from the center of pumping. Some of these wells are\x20
| located across a major topographic divide (Chapman,\x20
| 1999, p. 29; Tharpe and others, 1997), indicating that\x20
| bedrock fracture system supplying the wellfield is not\x20
| bounded by topographic basin divides. Well 13FF14,\x20
| the well farthest from Rhodes Jordan Wellfield that is\x20
| influenced by pumping, shows a drawdown in the range\x20
| from 0.5 ft to about 6 ft during longer periods of time.
| • Drawdown in bedrock wells varies with the duration,\x20
| rate of pumping, distance, and direction from the\x20
| Rhodes Jordan Wellfield. In well 14FF08, located about\x20
| 4,700 ft west of Rhodes Jordan Wellfield (fig. 1), the\x20
| water-level drawdown in response to cyclic weekly\x20
| pumping ranges from about 1 to 2 ft (fig. 8), with a long-
| term estimated drawdown8 from 4 to 5 ft. Other wells\x20
| responding to pumping at the Rhodes Jordan Wellfield\x20
| are listed in table 10.
| • The direction and magnitude of drawdown generally is\x20
| greater in the east-west direction parallel to bedrock\x20
| foliation and layering than in the north-south direction,\x20
| which is similar to the responses observed during aqui-
| fer tests described above. The preferential east-west\x20
| drawdown indicates that recharge and ground-water\x20
| flow probably is greatest along bedrock foliation planes\x20
| and compositional layering toward the pumped wells.8Long-term drawdown was estimated using a period of increased pumping from\x20

| Summary and Conclusions
| Large ground-water supplies needed for municipal and indus-
| trial use have been developed from igneous and metamorphic\x20
| crystalline rocks in many parts of the Piedmont and Blue Ridge\x20
| physiographic provinces. Little information generally is avail-
| able, however, to assess the potential of developing additional\x20
| ground-water supplies from these complex aquifer systems. The\x20
| U.S. Geological Survey, in cooperation with the City of\x20
| Lawrenceville, conducted this study to determine what geologic\x20
| structures, if any, contribute to the development of increased per-
| meability and high ground-water yield in the area.
| Thirty-two wells, ranging in depth from 180 to 630 feet (ft),\x20
| were used to evaluate the bedrock lithology, fracture, and water\x20
| yielding characteristics in the Lawrenceville area. The depth and\x20
| nature of the fractures were determined by directly observing\x20
| them in 32 open boreholes of wells with a borehole camera and\x20
| by using borehole-imaging and geophysical techniques. Yield\x20
| from individual water-bearing fractures was estimated by corre-
| lating increases in yield recorded during drilling to the measured\x20
| depths of these zones. Individual yield of water-bearing zones\x20
| was confirmed in 12 wells using flowmeter surveys. Ten aquifer\x20
| tests were conducted to determine longer-term yield from the\x20
| wells, which is the yield of all water-bearing fractures contribut-
| ing water to the borehole, and to determine the direction and mag-
| nitude of drawdown resulting from pumping. Packer tests were\x20
| conducted in one well to determine the hydraulic response among\x20
| discrete water-bearing fractures. Continuous water-level data\x20
| were collected from 26 wells to monitor the effects of rainfall,\x20
| stream stage, and pumping on water levels in the study area.
| Using borehole imaging data and correlating these to individ-
| ual fracture yield, two main systems of water-bearing zones were\x20
| identified: (1) joints, open joints, and zones of joint concentration\x20
| consisting mostly of steeply-dipping low-yielding fractures; and\x20
| (2) small and large openings along foliation planes and layering\x20
| consisting of discontinuous but high-yield fractures, which are\x20
| defined in this report as “foliation parallel-parting systems.”\x20
| Water-bearing zones also are formed from the dissolution of pre-
| existing crosscutting mineralized joints; these create irregular\x20
| openings that cut across foliation and layering. Chemical dissolu-
| tion along joints, foliation planes, and contacts between compo-
| sitional layers was apparent in several of the boreholes studied.
| Joints generally are complex “crisscross” fractures that break\x20
| up the rock into blocks and slabs of differing dimensions. In bore-
| holes, joints are differentiated from other fractures by their occur-
| rence in sets, common planar geometry, and their orientation cut-
| ting across rock foliation and layering. Most joints observed in\x20
| boreholes appeared as tight “hairline” fractures and typically did\x20
| not form significant water-bearing zones. Small to moderate\x20
| amounts of water—generally in the range from 1 to 5 gallons per\x20
| minute (gal/min)—were produced from open, steeply-dipping\x20
| joints and zones of joint concentration.

| JANUARY 1999DECEMBER 1998
| 1611 21 26 31 5 9Foliation partings and major foliation openings, which are\x20
| fractures formed nearly parallel to foliation and layering,\x20
| yielded large quantities of water to open boreholes in the\x20
| Lawrenceville area. Foliation partings, which are small open-
| ings typically less than 0.5-inch wide, yielded 1 to 15 gal/min,\x20
| with a maximum value of about 63 gal/min. In some boreholes,\x20
| groups of foliation partings formed substantial water-bearing\x20
| zones and yielded as much as 50 gal/min. Major foliation open-
| ings, which are large openings typically 1 to 8 inches wide,\x20
| yielded substantially more water than the smaller foliation part-
| ings. A range in yield from 50 to 100 gal/min is typical for these\x20
| larger fracture openings, making these the primary water-bear-
| ing features responsible for high-yield wells in the\x20
| Lawrenceville area. All the high-yield wells in the area pene-
| trate at least one major foliation opening.
| Dissolution openings along mineral infilled joints or veins\x20
| were observed in a few wells with air-lift yields of up to \x20
| 35 gal/min. This amount exceeds the typical range in yield for\x20
| open joints and zones of joint concentration, indicating the\x20
| importance of dissolution features in the bedrock. Dissolution\x20
| features may be more common than would be indicated by the\x20
| data collected because their identification requires a clear, high-
| quality image that shows remnants of the mineral infilling\x20
| around the edges of the joint or vein. These types of high-qual-
| ity images were not available in most of the wells studied.
| Flowmeter surveys confirmed the depth and yield contribu-
| tion from various types of water-bearing fractures in selected\x20
| wells, specifically the high-yielding nature of foliation partings\x20
| and major foliation openings. In several wells, flowmeter sur-
| veys helped to identify confined/semiconfined water-bearing\x20
| zones. Water from the confined/semiconfined zones flowed\x20
| from the fracture, up the borehole, and typically exited from the\x20
| borehole through shallower fractures. From these data, ground\x20
| water under ambient and pumping conditions appears to be\x20
| derived from discrete fractures intersecting the boreholes,\x20
| rather than being distributed evenly along the open interval.\x20
| Most of the inflow typically was concentrated within two to\x20
| three intervals, corresponding to the depth of water-bearing\x20
| fractures identified through other geophysical methods.
| Aquifer tests confirmed that production wells could sustain\x20
| large pumping rates for the period of the tests. Yields from aqui-
| fer tests ranged from 75 to 600 gal/min. In several wells, the\x20
| aquifer-test yield was substantially less than the reported air-lift\x20
| yield; whereas in other wells, the aquifer-test yield was greater\x20
| than the reported air-lift yield. The recovery of water levels fol-
| lowing pumping varied from slow, indicating a small recharge\x20
| rate to the fracture(s) supplying the well, to fast, indicating a\x20
text: default page 38
| References Cited 31large recharge rate to the fracture(s) supplying the well. Draw-
| down during the tests varied depending on the distance and\x20
| direction from the pumped well. In most of the aquifer tests, the\x20
| magnitude of drawdown was greater in the direction parallel to\x20
| bedrock foliation and layering than perpendicular to it. Aquifer\x20
| test results also indicated a strong potential for interferences\x20
| between certain wells. If pumped simultaneously, interferences\x20
| between wells likely would result in corresponding declines in\x20
| the pumping rates during long periods of time.
| Packer tests conducted in one well indicated that most of the\x20
| water enters the borehole through foliation openings and not\x20
| through joints, and confirms the high-yield nature of foliation\x20
| openings. A small amount of water probably enters through\x20
| joints, but this is much less than derived from the foliation open-
| ings tested. Drawdown responses from the packer tests also\x20
| indicated a slight but noticeable vertical connection among\x20
| individual high-yield foliation openings, likely resulting from\x20
| steeply-dipping joints.
| Ground-water levels in the study area fluctuate in response\x20
| to seasonal variations in rainfall, stream stage, and ground-
| water pumping. Open-hole water levels rise mostly above the\x20
| bottom of casing. Water levels measured in open boreholes rep-
| resent a composite potentiometric head of all the water-bearing\x20
| fractures. Six boreholes had water levels above land surface and\x20
| four of these flowed when left uncapped. Seasonal ground-
| water level fluctuations varied from about 1 to 3 ft in response\x20
| to variation in rainfall. Ground-water levels generally increased\x20
| during wet periods in response to recharge from infiltrating pre-
| cipitation and decreased during dry periods in response to\x20
| ground-water discharge to streams and evapotranspiration.\x20
| Variations in stream stage also may influence the water level in\x20
| at least one well. The water level in this well increased approx-
| imately 1 ft in response to the rise in stream stage.
| Water levels in about half of the wells used in this study\x20
| fluctuate in response to pumping at the Rhodes Jordan Well-
| field. In the immediate vicinity around the wellfield, drawdown\x20
| varies between 75 and 135 ft, depending on the rate and dura-
| tion of pumping. Lowering of the water level in the vicinity of\x20
| Rhodes Jordan Wellfield dewaters shallow water-bearing frac-
| tures but does not lower the water level in a nearby regolith\x20
| observation well, indicating poor hydraulic connection between\x20
| the bedrock and regolith at this location. Away from the well-
| field, drawdown in the bedrock was observed as far away as\x20
| 7,000 ft in wells located across a major topographic divide, indi-
| cating the bedrock fracture system supplying the wellfield is not\x20
| bounded by topographic basin divides. Drawdown is from\x20
| about 4 to 5 ft in one well, located 4,700 ft west of Rhodes\x20
| Jordan Wellfield. The magnitude and direction of drawdown\x20
| around the wellfield, is greater in the east-west direction, paral-
| lel to bedrock foliation and layering, than in the north-south\x20
| direction. The east-west drawdown indicates that recharge and\x20
| ground-water flow probably is concentrated along bedrock foli-
| ation planes and layering toward the pumped wells.
| Collectively, the data from this study indicate that foliation\x20
| parallel-parting systems, consisting of discontinuous zones of\x20
| foliation partings and major foliation openings, strongly influ-
| ence the yields of wells in the Lawrenceville area. Wells tap-
| ping these systems are capable of sustaining large ground-water\x20
| withdrawals for extended periods of time, as indicated from the\x20
| continuous operation of the Rhodes Jordan Wellfield since\x20
| 1995. Open-hole water levels, flowmeter surveys, and\x20
| preferential drawdown parallel to foliation and compositional\x20
| layering indicate a general hydraulic confinement of foliation\x20
| parallel-parting systems and indicate strong lithologic and\x20
| structural control on the development of these water-bearing\x20
| fracture systems.
| Foliation parallel-parting systems are easily identified in\x20
| boreholes using geophysical methods described in this report.\x20
| The yield potential of foliation parallel-parting systems within\x20
| an individual topographic basin or several topographic basins\x20
| can be large, depending on the areal extent of the water-
| bearing zones and the interconnectivity of these zones with\x20
| sources of recharge.

text: sorted page 13
| 6 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaTable 1. Description of principal lithologic units in the\x20
| Lawrenceville, Georgia, area (from Chapman and others, 1999).
| Amphibolite unit (a) – Fine- to medium-grained, dark green to\x20
| greenish-black, ocher weathering, massive to finely layered, locally\x20
| laminated, locally pillowed, locally chloritic, commonly garnetifer-
| ous, locally magnetite-bearing, generally pyrite-bearing, generally\x20
| epidotic, hornblende-plagioclase and plagioclase-hornblende\x20
| amphibolites with minor amounts (generally less than a very small\x20
| fraction of 1 percent) of fine- to medium-grained, generally amphib-
| ole-bearing, granofels. The final weathering product of the amphibo-
| lite is a very characteristic dark red clayey soil.
| Biotite Gneiss unit (bg) – Gray to grayish-brown to dark gray,\x20
| medium- to coarse-grained, commonly schistose, generally pegmati-
| tic (biotite-muscovite-quartz-potassium-feldspar pegmatites),\x20
| biotite-rich gneiss with generally rare but locally fairly common\x20
| layers, lenses, and pods of hornblende-plagioclase amphibolite.\x20
| Characteristically and commonly contains small pods and lenses of\x20
| altered meta-ultramafic rocks. The biotite gneiss weathers to a\x20
| uniform, slightly micaceous, dark-red saprolite and clayey dark red\x20
| soil; vermiculitic mica is characteristic of soils formed from the\x20
| biotite gneiss.
| Button Schist unit (bs) – Dark gray to brownish-gray, medium-\x20
| to coarse-grained, lustrous (where fresh), (± chlorite)-garnet-\x20
| biotite-muscovite-plagioclase-(± microcline)-quartz button schist\x20
| with tiny black opaques. In most outcrops the schist contains large\x20
| muscovite fish that weather to buttons. The button schist is resistant\x20
| to weathering.
| Diabase (dia) – Fine- to medium-grained, dark gray to black augite\x20
| diabase, in dikes generally 16 to 66 feet wide. The diabase weathers\x20
| to a dark red clayey soil containing speheroidal boulders with fresh\x20
| rock inside an armoring, ocherous rind.\x20
| Granite Gneiss unit (gg) – Complex of granite and granitic gneiss.\x20
| Light gray to whitish-gray, medium-grained muscovite-biotite-
| microcline-oligoclase-quartz gneiss having well defined gneissic\x20
| layering. Most commonly is poorly foliated. Pavement outcrops,\x20
| “whale-back” outcrops, and boulder outcrops are characteristic of\x20
| this granite gneiss. Where deeply weathered, the gneiss forms thin\x20
| light whitish-yellow sandy soils.
| Magnetite quartzite (mq) – Thinly layered (0.4 inch) to laminated,\x20
| medium-grained, magnetite quartzite in units about 1 to 20 feet\x20
| thick. Commonly has thin (from 0.4 to 1.6 inches) quartz-magnetite\x20
| layers, with magnetite crystals as much as 0.4 inch in size, but com-
| monly about 0.04 inch. The quartz-magnetite layers alternate with\x20
| quartz layers without magnetite, or quartz layers with a small per-
| centage of magnetite, from about 1.6 to 3.2 inches thick. Magnetite\x20
| clumps that generally disrupt the layering are locally as large as\x20
| 8 inches, but are commonly about 0.4 inch.
| Quartzite/Schist unit (qs) – White to yellowish, sugary, to vitreous,\x20
| slightly graphitic to nongraphitic quartzite with accessory musco-
| vite, garnet and aluminosilicate minerals (kyanite, staurolite, or silli-
| manite), in layers from about 1 to 4 feet thick, interlayered with\x20
| feldspathic quartzite and garnetiferous quartz-muscovite or musco-
| vite-quartz schist. The aluminous schist part of the unit is commonly\x20
| a tan to yellow weathering, sheared or button-textured, commonly\x20
| quartzose, garnet-biotite-plagioclase-muscovite-quartz schist that\x20
| generally contains kyanite or staurolite.Well-Naming System
| In this report, wells are named using a system based on USGS\x20
| 7½-minute topographic quadrangle maps. Each topographic map\x20
| in Georgia has been assigned a number and letter designation begin-
| ning at the southwestern corner of the State. Numbers increase east-
| ward through 39; letters advance northward through “Z,” then double-
| letter designations “AA” through “PP” are used. The letters “I,”\x20
| “O,” “II,” and “OO” are not used. Wells inventoried in each quad-
| rangle are named sequentially beginning with “1.” Thus, the 55th\x20
| well inventoried in the Lawrenceville 7.5 minute quadrangle (des-
| ignated 14FF) in Gwinnett County is designated as well 14FF55.
| Supplemental Data on CD–ROM
| Supplemental data on this CD–ROM include detailed lithol-
| ogy, fracture, yield, and water-level data collected at various wells\x20
| in the study area. These data are presented in a nonproprietary\x20
| Geographic Information System (GIS) database file format that\x20
| includes geographic and tabular data. A copy of ArcExplorer®, a\x20
| free GIS data viewer developed by Environmental Systems\x20
| Research Institute, is included on this CD–ROM for use with the\x20
| GIS database and allows a user to view the data spatially and query\x20
| the data of interest. In addition, much of the data are linked to a\x20
| hypertext markup language (HTML) document to allow a user to\x20
| access and view the data tables through text and Adobe® Portable\x20
| Document Format (PDF) files. A description of the GIS data and\x20
| figures contained on this CD–ROM is provided in the section\x20
| “Organization and Presentation of Hydrogeologic Data.”

| 12 Methods and Hydrogeologic Data in Lawrenceville, Georgia, AreaCharacterization of Fractures in Open Boreholes The orientation (strike and dip) of fractures was determined\x20
| from the ATV and BIPS logs. The strike and dip indicate the\x20
